# 2203475-Ways_of_blood_and_of_spirit_v1.pdf

 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
1 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
The·Ways·of·Blood 
Magic·and·Spirit 
 
 
Dark Ages Britain. The Spring of 495CE. 
There is rejoicing as the love of Christianity waxes in the north-
ern province of Gwynedd. 
However, the jubilation of the righteous is tempered by the 
suffering of the displaced. A sickness stalks the land. Crops are 
failing, the people are becoming ill with plague after plague, and 
more worrying, at this rate there will be no births next year. 
The populace has taken to calling it "The Blight".  
The Adventurers who seek to deal with The Blight may be Am-
baxtos Warriors, Christian Ascetics, Bards, Druids, Hunters, 
Outlaws, Lyblæca Sorcerers, Saxon Ċeorls, Traders, and even 
Tenant Farmers - they all have the common goal to restore the 
land.  
Using the Ennie Award winning Forbidden Lands System 
mechanics, the players will explore Dark Ages history, experi-
ence Arthurian legend, meet Welsh folklore head on, and over-
come dreadful adversity, using wit, guile, and force of arms. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
2 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Introduction 
Aeddan narrowed his eyes, staring at the incoming bank of roiling clouds, and pulling his thick felted poncho closer 
around his shoulder. He turned back toward his companion, the sound of rhythmic grinding overwhelming her muttered 
chanting. "Beren, why are you kindling a Need-Fire at this ill-omened time? A storm is coming… 
although I think we will not see any gods-blessed rain from it". The slight girl crouching in front of him 
spared him no more than a disgusted glance before turning back to her improvised fire-bow. Eventually she stopped 
pulling on the bow, encased a smoking nugget in tinder with a quick flick of her fingers, and blew gently until there 
was a soft crackle and then flames and smoke wreathed her hands. She thrust the bundle aloft, oblivious to the crackling 
heat, calling out, "Andraste heed my call!", before carefully pushing the blazing tinder into the prepared fire.   
As the flames leapt and danced in the gusting wind, Aeddan glanced around nervously at the tall oaks that surrounded 
them and drummed his fingers on his spear-shaft. "I hear there are Bwbachod in these woods. Maybe the 
storm will keep the foul beasts in their little crevices. It's not like we have anything shiny to offer 
them".  Beren cocked her head to one side, as if listening intently. Aeddan's heart sank, as he made out over the wind 
the unmistakable chattering laugh of a Bwback.  Beren beamed, "The auguries were right after all..." 
 
This document provides a full campaign that allows you and your players to explore a Dark Ages North 
Wales in the time of Arthur as it might have been. It colours between the lines of what we know and 
provides an exciting and fanciful adventure in a format that is easy to run and compelling to play. 
It also can be used as a Campaign Setting in its own right; ignore the story and set up your own sandbox in 
this fascinating era. A host of tools are provided, and the Forbidden Lands ruleset is already well adapted 
to sandbox play. 
Content Warning and Safety Tools 
The Ways of Blood, Magic and Spirit is set in a brutal past; however, the GM can tailor the content to suit 
their table and preferences. The core plot elements, as written, include Child Grooming, off-screen Human 
Sacrifice, off-screen harm to Children, Deprivation, Torture, Violence, and a less than flattering portrayal 
of real-world Religion. 
As Game Master (GM), you should be mindful of the possible triggers and discomfort these scenes might 
present and provide a mechanism to pause or cut scenes, or avoid difficult subjects, as necessary. 
Also please consider using John Stavropoulos' X-Card, Sean Reynolds and Shanna Germain’s Lines and 
Veils, “Consent in Gaming” guide, and other relevant tools. 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
3 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
How to use this document 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit isn’t a story because it 
hasn’t been told yet. What emerges at your table, however, will be a 
story – one that you and your players will tell. This document is de-
signed to help you develop that compelling narrative and in as simple 
and easy manner as possible.  
It is designed to be compatible with Fria Ligan’s Forbidden Lands 
system mechanics, but it can be adjusted to suit other systems1 that 
would work in a Dark Ages setting with Magic. 
The intent is to gather relevant information in easy to find places, 
cross-reference it so that you can quickly jump to it, and then only 
give you the information you need, when you need it. This bare-
bones approach facilitates significantly minimising GM preparation 
time; giving the GM the flexibility and freedom to adjust the story on 
the fly, yet it allows a rich, detailed, and consistent narrative to de-
velop. This necessarily means that there will be supplemental infor-
mation for the GM to fill in, but crucially, the type of information 
that is difficult to create on-the-fly is provided and is easy to find.  
The design is modular, so it can be run with: a) all the details in the Setting Guide, b) a stripped-down version 
with all the fun-bits included but without the need to include lots of historical details, or c) the GM going 
all-in, looking up information in the wealth of hyperlinks and providing a deeply immersive gaming expe-
rience. 
Document Layout 
• 
Player Guide (pg 8)  – full details to allow the Players to build and equip their Character. This entire 
section, pgs. 8 through 29,  can be freely provided to the players. 
• 
The Framework (pg 30) – top level details of what the game is about and what kind of story may 
develop. 
• 
GM Mood Prompts (Pg 34) – short descriptions to help bring the world to life for the GM and 
players.  
• 
Locations (Pg 37) – general information on different types of sites and then detailed, cross-refer-
enced tables providing information on key locations. Some maps and plans are provided, but these 
are found in the Obstacles section, when relevant. Otherwise, detailed maps of many locations are 
not provided, so the GM can adjust these as needed for their table of players. 
• 
Significant NPCs (pg 58) – NPC relationship charts are provided for Significant and Minor NPCs, 
and full details and stats are provided for the Significant NPCs, fully cross-referenced. 
• 
Obstacles (Pg 78) – using Robin D Law’s Sharper Adventures method, the Campaign is organised 
into a series of Obstacles. The Obstacles are modular and reusable and what obstacles are experi-
enced depends on the decisions made by the Players.  
• 
Setting Guide (Pg 144) – all the details needed to bring Dark Ages Gwynedd to life. 
 
1 Such as Mythras Mythic Britain, Keltia, Wolves of God, and others.

4 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Document Navigation 
To aid identification and speed navigation about the document, the following format is used.  
• 
For Locations there is a hyperlinked reference, and the name is underlined, e.g. L1.1 Aberdaron.  
• 
For Significant NPCs the name is in bold followed by a subscript hyperlink, e.g. Andronicus(C1.1). 
• 
For Obstacles the Obstacle number is listed and the whole reference is italicized, followed by a 
page number. E.g. O01. Augury on Moel Yscydion (pg. 82). 
• 
For Artefacts a reference in brackets is used, e.g. Cernunnos’ Torc (A02). 
• 
External hyperlinks, use default underlined blue, e.g. Wikipedia. 
• 
Otherwise, Internal hyperlinks are also italicised, usually with a page number, e.g. Introduction (pg. 
2). 
It is highly recommended that you activate your PDF viewer’s Navigation Buttons. That way you can 
click on hyperlinks, read the relevant text, and then leap back to your original position with one click. 
Note: A high-resolution version of the Gwynedd Hex Map and an image suitable to use for a glossy front 
and back cover when using a Print on Demand service are both available at https://dosmania.itch.io/. 
The Sharper Adventures Method2 
1. Define the group’s Core Activity – here the group’s goal is to remove The Blight. 
2. Establish Emotional Stakes – these are largely defined at Character Generation. 
3. Structure the adventure with a Premise and a Resolution, possibly connected by act breaks: the 
Point of No Return and the Escalation – See the Obstacles. 
4. Connect these points with Obstacles, consisting of: 
a) Dilemma 
b) Choices 
c) Consequences 
d) Rooting Interest 
 
 
 
2 For more information see Robin D Laws, Kraken Chapbooks: “Sharper Adventures in HeroQuest Glorantha”, 2014, and 
“Adventure Crucible, Building Stronger Scenarios for Any RPG”, 2023. 
GM Note: The Campaign is designed to represent a gritty and realistic portrayal of Dark Ages life in 
Britain, given current knowledge and the constraints of an RPG. Encounters have not been balanced, and 
the default means to succeed should not be by hitting things, although that will often be necessary. 
Therefore, the GM should adjust the number of adversaries and their advantages to their group and 
situation. The GM can, of course, balance the encounters themselves and adjust the style of play from 
Pulp, to Intrigue, to Whatever take their fancy.  
Finally, before allowing Players to choose the Christian Ascetic profession, the GM should ensure that 
the player understands that this might lead to internal conflict with the other PCs. This can be a rich 
source of Role Play, but it is possibly not a good choice for a group with less experienced players.

Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Player Guide 
Player Characters’ Briefing 
Early Spring, 495 CE, Cantref of Dunoding, Kingdoms of Gwynedd, Britain.  
The Kingdoms of Gwynedd in the Utter West of Britain are lands tended by poor farmers, ruled by petty 
kings and their mustered warbands, divided by religion, and rife with superstition. The Otherworld of 
Annwn is accepted by the Pagans as real, although many Christians scoff at the idea. However, Beings of 
Magic, such as Bwbachod (Goblins) and Cewri (Giants), can and do impact on everyday life.   
Until last year life went on in Gwynedd pretty much like it did for your fathers and for their fathers. The 
Gaels have been here since anyone can remember, and the small number of Saxons, who increasingly are 
demonstrating their might and power, first started arriving in your grandparents’ time. It was pleasingly 
stable.   
However, last year things changed. A sickness now stalks the land of Gwynedd. The harvest failed utterly, 
the winter was harsh, the people are becoming ill with new plagues, and more worryingly, pregnancies are 
dwindling. At this rate there will be no births next year. In the trefs and hillforts it is being called The 
Blight. 
You are a majority Briton, or a minority Saxon or Gael. It matters not whether you are a visitor or a long-
standing local, as long as you have a Motivation to deal with The Blight. 
Common knowledge in Dunoding Cantref 
• 
The Rhi is Dingad ab Eifion. His warband is smaller than Cadwallon’s in neighbouring Arfon 
Cantref and less disciplined than that of the Christian Einion in Afflogion Cantref, but Dingad 
maintains a nearly equal footing and has a good relationship with Cadwallon. 
• 
Everyone knows that the druids hold great power in Gwynedd, but they use it subtly and generally 
act as advisors, rather than use displays of potency. Thus far, they have not been seen to be reacting 
to The Blight. 
• 
Coins have not been minted in living memory, so payment in hard currency is unusual and their 
liquidity, compared to bartering, is extremely desirable. 
• 
Dingad and Einon don’t get on, however, the Pagan Rhi Cadwallon appears to be trying to mend 
his relationship with the Christians, which may affect political alliances with Dingad. Rumour has 
it that he has sent his son to be educated by the Cenobites in the north of Arfon. 
• 
Arthur and his Knights are far enough from these lands to exist in story only, although travellers 
often bring news of Arthur’s supposed exploits against the Saxons. 
• 
The fight for basic subsistence in the face of The Blight has brought the Cultures into Conflict. 
The rich Saxon Ċeorls and the poorer Eillt Farmers have clashed already over the distribution of 
food and fair payment of taxes3. It is only a matter of time before the discord spreads and before 
warriors will be found reiving livestock from neighbouring Cantrefs. Conflict: Violence vs Star-
vation. 
 
3 Taxes here are the “treth” and are a mix of perishable (ale, honey, meat, and cheese) and less-perishable (grain, wool, 
linen, underwood, timber, and crafted items) goods. Livestock is the principal unit of currency, partially due to the 
ease of transport. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
9 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
In Media Res… the beginning 
It has been a cold and arid spring in the Kingdoms of Gwynedd. You have been tasked4 by the Rhi of 
Dunoding cantref, Dingad ab Eifion, to escort his head Druid from Gaer Dolbenmaen, along the coast, 
and into the high mountains to the south of the cantref. There you were to ascend to the grave cairn of the 
giant Yscydion Gawr and assist this druid, Elno, with his auguries there. You are to be paid in silver on 
your return, that is, if you are successful.  
To ascend to the mountain tops, the domain of the gods, is something only a druid would dare, furthermore 
you are notionally outside of the borders of Dunoding now. However, you have put your reservations 
behind you and here you are. 
Elno has been tasked by the Rhi with understanding the source of the scourge of the land that the people 
of Dunoding have taken to calling “The Blight”. This term encompasses the failing crops, new sicknesses 
rife amongst the farmers, and a plummeting birth rate. By this time next year, you expect no new births in 
Gwynedd.  
Elno is phlegmatic about his chances, but regardless, you have all travelled for three days through difficult 
terrain and even had to, ahem, “borrow” a boat to cross a rushing river below Moel Yscydion. After a cold 
camp at the foot of the mountain, the climb to the giant’s grave was punishing and, as you ascended into 
mist and murk, you felt a sense of menace fall on your shoulders and the hairs on the back of your neck 
prickle.  
Suddenly, through the cold fog a single mournful howl was heard, then joined by another, and then another, 
and then all was silent except for the crunching of gravel under your feet. The fog seemed to play tricks 
with your ears, and you found it impossible to work out the distance to the howling creatures. 
“Here we are, just over this hump. Those wolves will keep their distance whilst the light holds, there’s too many of us.”, said 
Elno carefully, as he looked back at you, “Are you coming? We’d best get this over with.”  
 
 
 
 
4 You have carried out several small tasks thus far for the Rhi, and currently enjoy free access to the Functions of his 
Royal Court, Gaer Dolbenmaen. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
10 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Your Adventurer 
Use the Forbidden Lands character generation method (Forbidden Lands Players Guide (FLPG) pg 15), 
except:  
• 
Instead of a Kin, choose your Culture (Briton, Saxon, or Gael) and Religion (Saxon Pagan, Celtic 
Pagan, or Christian).  
• 
Age Ranges (FLPG pg 31) should be adjusted to Young 13-20 / Adult 21-35 / Old 36+, which 
reflects the short lifespan and early maturity of a denizen of Dark Ages British Isles. 
• 
Additionally create a Motivation which will drive your character forward in the narrative.  
Britons are the local dominant Celtic culture, Saxons, the elite immigrants from the east, and Gaels are 
Hibernian (Irish) Celtic immigrants. The GM can provide a more complete list of suggested names and 
epithets and guides on pronunciation for the three cultures. 
Note: Certain Skills, Talents and Magic in the Forbidden Lands system may need some modification or 
should be avoided (e.g. Bows are used for hunting only at this time, so the Bowyer talent is less useful than 
might be first thought. Similarly Hammer Fighter and Sharpshooter talents do not provide benefits, unless 
modified by a House Rule). If you find that this invalidates your planned character build, then please discuss 
with the GM. 
Example PC Motivations 
Kin – your family are starving and won’t accept charity, The Blight’s effect on them has made it personal. 
Love – your intended has fallen ill and now is listless and weakening by the day. You must find and stop 
The Blight before it is too late. 
Fear – if you don’t deal with The Blight, you fear that you will lose everything that you hold dear. 
Faith – The Blight stands in contrast to everything you believe in, if you don’t deal with it, then what does 
that mean for your faith? 
Hate – You seethe with rage at the Rhi’s ineffectual measures to deal with The Blight. His tenants provide 
their taxes, yet the Rhi does nothing. Perhaps, if you sort the problem, then you will be able to sleep more 
easily.  
Greed – you have offered your services to the Rhi and he will pay you handsomely if you can deal with 
The Blight. 
Pride – whilst under the influence of a little too much mead, you boasted at the recent Feast of Brigid that 
you would remove the problem of The Blight. Now you need to deal with it to save face. 
Honour – your Rhi has entrusted you with removing the Blight and you have vowed to complete your 
mission. Your honour demands that you fulfil your pledge. 
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
11 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Culture (Kin)  
“Men and women belonged to families; families to their kin, sept or pagus; kin and sept to their tribes, each with its totem – 
boar, goat, stag, bear. They looked to their legendary progenitor, a warrior or god or combination thereof, for aid and inspiration. 
Their mythological origins lay obscurely among generations buried under dark mounds in the days when ruined walls and cities, 
built by a lost race of giants, had stood proud and tall.” ‘The First Kingdom’, by Max Adams 
Britons 
“To the brave belong all things.” The Brittonic Celts are the majority population in the Kingdom of Gwynedd, 
having settled the area sometime between 500 and 200 BCE. They are a proud, generous, and hot-headed 
people, tall and well-built generally, with pale skin and hair colour tending towards red, auburn, and blond 
rather than brown or black. The Celts were previously uniformly Pagan, but even this far-flung province 
has begun to see a rising tide of Christianity, after missionaries arrived in the 4th century. 
Key Attribute: Strength 
Culture Talent: choose between Adaptive and True Grit 
Typical Professions: Aillt Farmer, Ambaxtos Warrior, Bard, Christian Ascetic, Druid, Hunter, Outlaw 
Typical Female Given Names: Arianell, Efa, Bronwen, Gwenddwyn, Rhosyn 
Typical Male Given Names: Brân, Conan, Gareth, Morvyn, Uthr  
Language: Brythonic 
For Brittonic names use ‘ab’ and ‘ferch for son and daughter, or use an Epithet (e.g. …the Strong). So, for 
males use Given Name “ab” then father’s Given Name, e.g. “Conan ab Gareth”. For females it is “ferch” then 
father’s name, e.g “Efa ferch Gareth”. 
Saxons 
“Let whoever can, win glory before death. When a warrior is gone, that will be his best and only bulwark” The Saxons are 
newcomers, arriving in Britain in the 5th Century after the departure of the Romans. Their culture resulted 
from the mixing of incoming Germanic peoples with the Britons. The Saxons tend to value heroic traits, 
such as bravery, perseverance, sacrifice, loyalty, duty to the tribe and king, and honour. They hold kingdoms 
in the east, but here in the west their presence is that of a minority elite; they are not tenant farmers or 
vassals of the king, but free men and women. Like the Celts they are beginning to be converted to Christi-
anity, but the majority remain Pagan, although worshipping a different pantheon. 
Key Attribute: Wits 
Culture Talent: Adaptive 
Typical Professions: Christian Ascetic, Lyblæca Sorcerer, Outlaw, Ċeorl, Trader. 
Typical Female Given Names: Aelfrith, Cyst, Edyth, Moira, Rowenna 
Typical Male Given Names: Aethelfrith, Eomon, Godred, Raedward, Waelfwulf 
Language: Old English and Brythonic 
For Saxon names in Gwynedd use a Saxon Given Name plus a Brythonic Epithet, e.g. Edyth the Honest. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
12 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Gaels 
The Gaels, Hibernian (i.e. Irish) Celts, are also not native to Gwynedd, having travelled over the water from 
Cóiced Laigen in Hibernia (Ireland). Although culturally and anatomically very similar to the Britons, the 
Gaels are genetically distinct and have their own language. Their Pagan pantheon overlaps that of the Brit-
tonic Celts to the extent that they can be considered the same here. 
Key Attribute: Empathy 
Culture Talent: Adaptive 
Typical Professions: Aillt Farmer, Ambaxtos Warrior, Bard, Christian Ascetic, Druid, Hunter, Outlaw 
Typical Female Names: Aífe, Derbáil, Eithne, Gormlaith, Rathnat 
Typical Male Names: Áedán, Berach, Cáelfind, Fergal, Niall 
Language: Primitive Irish and Brythonic 
For Gaelic names in Gwynedd use the Given Name with a Brythonic Epithet, e.g. Áedán the Faithful. 
Note: Here for simplicity, we are using Old Irish, rather than Primitive Irish names. 
Religion 
With the exception that Druids must be Pagan and Ascetics must be Christian, the PCs may choose either 
Saxon Pagan, Celtic Pagan (Briton and Gael), or the Christian religion. 
 
 
 
 
The Wild Hunt, led by Gwyn ap Nudd 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
13 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Professions 
Aillt Farmer 
The Eillt (s. Aillt) are tenant farmers, they work the land and pay their taxes (render) to the Rhi in terms of 
cereals, livestock, and maybe honey. They may not be expert warriors like the Ambaxtos, but they can still 
wield a spear and shake shield, and their skills with animals, ability to craft, and treat injuries, both animal 
and human, can prove very useful to a party of adventurers.  
Key Attributes: Strength 
Skills: Animal Handling, Crafting, Endurance, Healing, Melee,  
Talents: Path of the Beast, Path of Many Things, Path of the Plains 
Typical Epithets: Steadfast, Bull’s Blood, Evergreed 
Example Prides: 
• 
Your hoe arm is as strong as anyone’s. 
• 
What you don’t know about growing things and raising livestock could be kept in the smallest cup 
in your household. 
• 
You don’t need help from anyone, especially those who think they are your betters. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
Making offerings to the Christian god has kept you from starving, but you dare not let your neigh-
bours realise you have converted. 
• 
You took only that comely lass / handsome man for a tumble and a bit of fun. Only they are 
besotted now; and you are so far below their station. If their father finds out, you are in for it! 
• 
Your debts are mounting to the Rhi. Indenture is in your future if you can’t sort this soon!  
Example Relationships:  
• 
…is good with beasts of burden, perhaps you can guide them further? 
• 
…is greedy and takes more than is their due. You can show them the middle path by demonstrating 
how a true aillt operates. 
• 
…knows nothing of farming the land. They don’t deserve your time or guidance. 
Gear: Spear, Celtic Shield, One item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D12 silver 
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D8 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
14 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Ambaxtos Warrior 
As a trusted member of the warband and vassal to the Rhi, 
an Ambaxtos demands the respect due an adept in the mar-
tial arts.  
A Celtic warrior uses intimidation and spectacle to prevail, 
as much or more than they use force of arms. Therefore, 
appearance is as important as prowess.  
The warrior’s hair is either braided or bleached with lime 
and spiked, whilst the men shave their bodies to facilitate 
skin decoration. Male Ambaxtos will sport either a full 
beard, or the higher ranks may shave their cheeks, leaving a heavy moustache. All will decorate their skin 
with either painted designs or permanent tattoos.  
Battle trophies can include the heads of fallen enemies5 or just a necklace made with chunks of iron from 
the spears of bested foes. 
The Ambaxtos do more than maintain the Rhi’s power, they enforce his laws and collect his taxes at harvest.  
Their strength, ability to intimidate, and endurance give backbone to an adventuring party. 
Key Attributes: Strength 
Skills: Endurance, Might, Melee, Performance, Move 
Talents: Path of the Blade, Path of the Enemy, Path of the Shield 
Typical Epithets: Wearyless, Iron Arm, Faithful 
Example Prides: 
• 
Your Rhi relies upon you above all others. 
• 
Pain does not affect you like other, weaker, people. 
• 
You feel no fear in dangerous situations. 
Example Dark Secrets:  
• 
You fled your last Rhi’s service after bedding their son or daughter. 
• 
Inflicting pain on others gives you a positive feeling you can’t get any other way. 
• 
Killing causes you to become physically ill. You’ve managed to hide this thus far. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…is the weak link in the group. Can you teach them? 
• 
…has saved your life several times. How will you repay them? 
• 
…is creepy. They must be up to no good. 
Gear: A Sword or Spear, Leather Tunic armour, One item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, 
D6 Silver. 
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D6 
 
 
5 “Celts hanging severed heads from the necks of their horses while riding in order to intimidate onlookers and enemies 
alike” 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
15 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Bard 
What holds society together? It is the bards, an informal Celtic order of minstrel-poets. The bards’ main 
function is to compose verses celebrating the exploits of chiefs and warriors, and their patron may expect 
them to launch into The War Song of Cunedda or The Death Song of Gwyn ap Nudd at a moment’s notice. 
However, more importantly, they are also the foundation of the Celtic oral tradition by committing to verse 
historical and traditional facts, religious precepts, laws, and genealogies. Their knowledge of lore, ability to 
read a person and influence them, as well as their healing abilities, make them an important part of any 
group of adventurers. 
Key Attributes: Empathy 
Skills: Lore, Insight, Manipulation, Performance, Healing 
Talents: Path of the Hymn, Path of the Song, Path of the Warcry, Path of the Vicious Lampoon (new) 
Typical Epithets: Silvertongue, Honey voice, Excitable 
Example Prides: 
• 
Society cannot function without you providing the foundation of the oral tradition. 
• 
Your songs can bring ease and happiness to even the most downtrodden Aillt farmer. 
• 
Your knowledge of the oral history of Gwynedd is infallible. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
Your best-loved creations have all be stolen from other, better bards. 
• 
You were hounded out of a nearby community after having an affair with an important member 
of the community. You barely escaped with your life. 
• 
You are secretly deeply in love with an NPC or another PC. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…is the hero of your next creation, but they do not realise it. 
• 
…does not understand the ways of the world, but you can help them. 
• 
…is doomed. It is sad, but it is only a matter of time. 
Gear: Crwth (Lyre) or Pibgorn (pipe-horn), knife, one item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, 
D8 Silver. 
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D6 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
16 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Ċeorl 
A Ċeorl, or freeman, represents a minority elite in Gwynedd. Farther east the Saxons hold sway, but here a 
Ċeorl must bide their tongue and take comfort in their superiority and greater wealth over their neighbours. 
A Ċeorl may manage a sizeable estate yet be expected to “get their hands dirty” when it comes to raising 
livestock and harvesting grain. Their skills combine those of a farmer, some martial ability, with a knack for 
managing people effectively. 
Key Attributes: Empathy 
Skills: Animal Handling, Crafting, Insight, Melee, Manipulation,  
Talents: Path of Treasure, Path of Words, Path of the Companion 
Typical Epithets: Silver Pouch, Slow Reader, High Shield 
Example Prides: 
• 
You and your kind are superior to the Celts in almost every way. 
• 
Your hide of land is more fruitful than all your neighbours. 
• 
Your sword-arm is the strongest amongst the Ċeorls. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
You beat your slaves like everyone else, but you take a special pleasure in the act. 
• 
Rather than pay the werġild for a wrong done to a neighbour, you fled from Mercia and restarted 
your life in Gwynedd. 
• 
You practice the dark rites of Christianity, though outwardly you pretend to follow Woden, Tiw, 
or another pagan god. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…doesn’t understand the natural order of things. There are those on top, like yourself, and those 
who serve. 
• 
…is quite efficient, it’s time you convinced them to join your hide. 
• 
…knows that you have been short-changing your Rhi in the taxes.  
Gear: A Sword or Spear, Saxon Shield, One item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D12 silver 
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D8 
 
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
17 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Christian Ascetic  
[Warning: Discuss with your GM before choosing this Profession] 
Christian Ascetics have been a feature of Britain since the missionaries brought their religion here more 
than a century ago, especially Christian Hermits; however, ascetics such as Monks and Nuns are new feature. 
An Ascetic brings different skills to bear than a musclebound warrior or practical farmer; they understand 
people, know much about the land, have healing skills, and are able to get what they want out of others 
much of the time. Some will choose the life of a hermit and be able to subsist on little food and water – 
eventually these hermits achieve sainthood and are able to call their God for divine intervention, although 
in a limited manner compared to Druids and Lyblæca Sorcerers.  
Key Attributes: Empathy 
Skills: Insight, Healing, Lore, Manipulation, Performance 
Talents: Path of the Hymn, Path of Words, Path of the Saints (new) 
Typical Epithets: Charity, Righteous, Voice of God 
Example Prides: 
• 
Only you can convert these heathen pagans and bring them to the light. 
• 
The light of God burns more brightly in you than in your peers. 
• 
Your Christianity gives you special knowledge that sets you apart from pagans.  
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
You don’t confess all your sins or even most of them. 
• 
You often breach your order’s tenets when no one is looking. 
• 
You find it impossible not to impose your religious ideals on everyone around you. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
...is a prime target for conversion to the One faith. 
• 
…sins from the moment they wake until they fall asleep. The question is whether to help them or 
damn them. 
• 
…is suspicious of your motives. You must show them that your motivation is pure.   
Gear: Staff or knife, one item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D6 Silver.  
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D8 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
18 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Druid  
The druids are the link between the Celtic people and their pagan gods. Though much diminished since 
their massacre at the hands of the Romans in Gwynedd in the 1st century CE, the druids are still core to 
Celtic society and a high-ranking priestly class. Some are performers, some politicians and some are schem-
ers. 
Amongst pagans the Druids are responsible for organizing worship and sacrifices, divination, and judicial 
procedures; their censure can make a pagan an outcast, and their opinions hold great weight amongst even 
the Rhiau. Their skills are a mix of the practical and the intellectual, and their ability to bend reality itself 
and to augur the future mean they are an indispensable part of any adventuring party.  
Their tenets are kept secret from non-druids; if a druid breaks these tenets, they will face the sanction of 
their peers. High-status Druids command great respect: they can wander into a battlefield unmolested, 
common folk will not dare to lift a hand against them, and high-status Druids can excommunicate and 
curse pagans, a trait that makes them doubly feared. See Druidic Tenets and Reputation (pg 24). 
Like all those who touch the otherworld, druids appear to be a bit odd. Their minds work differently, they 
struggle to relate to normal folk, and suddenly exclaiming “Brigid’s Bountiful Breasts” and jumping on the 
table whilst spitting into a pot, tends to disturb the neighbours.   
Key Attributes: Wits 
Skills: Endurance, Survival, Insight, Healing, Animal Handling 
Talents: Path of Healing, Path of Shifting Shapes, Path of Sight, Path of the Vicious Lampoon (new) 
Typical Epithets: Windwalker, Storm Chaser, Ravenwing 
Example Prides: 
• 
You understand the true ways of the world and are blessed by the gods. 
• 
You have a special rapport with the beasts of Gwynned. 
• 
Christians instinctively fear you. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
You relish excommunicating those who you feel have wronged you. 
• 
You sometimes intone false prophecies to get what you want. 
• 
You often reveal sacred druidic secrets to laypeople to impress them. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…shows signs of enjoying killing a bit too much. They will need careful watching. 
• 
…is troubled, but with the right guidance could become someone great. 
• 
…has somehow learned some of the druid’s secret tenets. You may need to stop them learning 
more, or your Order may take the matter into its own hands.  
Gear: Staff or knife, one item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D6 Silver.  
Resource Dice: Food D8, Water D8 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
19 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Hunter 
Although the wealth of the land is largely reaped via the Eillt farmers, Hunters also have a key role in 
Gwynedd. Most of Gwynedd is forested, rather than farmland, and Hunters roam the woods, bringing back 
food for the pot. Additionally, the Rhiau and Uchelwyr will occasionally employ hunters when stalking stags 
for sport. Now that The Blight has destroyed the recent harvest, the role of the Hunter is even more 
important.  
Key Attributes: Agility 
Skills: Stealth, Move, Marksmanship, Scouting, Survival 
Talents: Path of the Sling (new), Path of the Beast, Path of the Forest 
Typical Epithets: Swiftheart, Silentfoot, Provider 
Example Prides: 
• 
You can survive on next to nothing and thrive in the forest. 
• 
Your sling stones are uncannily accurate. 
• 
The beasts of the woods trust you. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
You feel uncomfortable when in a Tref. It is much better to be alone on the hunt. 
• 
You saw something otherworldly once. It changed you and you have never felt at ease ever since. 
• 
Once, when you were faced with a raging bear, you fled and left your companion to her fate. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…lumbers through the forest like a fat trader. Beware of her. 
• 
…has an appetite that is only matched by her inability to catch anything. 
• 
…heeds not the rhythm of the forest, it will come back to bite her. 
Gear: Sling, two items of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D6 Silver. 
Resource Dice: Food D6, Water D8, Sling Stones D10 
Note: Hunters with the Path of the Beast Talent may employ an Agassian hunting dog. They are small in 
size, but endowed with powerful claws, sharp teeth, and have a marvellous ability to track prey by their 
scent. 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
20 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Lyblæca Sorcerer 
A Lyblæca is a Saxon sorcerer. The rigours of dredging up the will to bend reality take their toll, and Lyblæca 
are usually readily differentiated from their mundane counterparts. Maybe their pupils are the wrong shape, 
or their skin or hair an odd pallor, or perhaps they always seem to be looking over your left shoulder when 
they talk to you, but something is different. Amongst their own Saxon kin, they are hated and feared; 
perhaps sought out for love potions and cure-alls when in great need, but otherwise distrusted. However, 
in Gwynedd their differences may be less obvious to these Celts, and they may be able to hide their calling, 
for a while anyway. 
Lyblæca minds work differently; they struggle to relate to normal folk, and suddenly exclaiming “Woden’s 
Enormous Knackers” and diving under the table whilst shrieking a cry of triumph, will cause a few tongues to 
wag.   
Key Attributes: Wits 
Skills: Crafting, Sleight of Hand, Lore, Insight, Manipulation 
Talents: Path of Blood, Path of Death, Path of Signs, Path of Stone 
Typical Epithets: Rootheart, Greycape, The Wise 
Example Prides: 
• 
Fear is for the mundanes who have not seen through the veil, like you have. 
• 
Anyone who crosses you risks a pitiful and painful death. 
• 
Your knowledge of the world exceeds those petty people around you. 
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
Seeing through the veil has left you haunted with terrifying visions. 
• 
Your need for dark power is insatiable. 
• 
The druids and their groves are an anathema for you – you know the spirits will catch your spoor 
and come for their reckoning. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…is superstitious and you can tell that they don’t trust you nor your power. 
• 
…has a spiritual strength that you could nurture and grow. 
• 
…is ignorant of the dark powers, you will show them something to fear! 
Gear: Staff or Knife, One item of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, D8 Silver. 
Resource Dice: Food D6, Water D8 
Special Rule: Such is the suspicion and prejudice against the Lyblæca, that a Lyblæca who attempts a 
Manipulation test against a Briton or Gael who knows of their arcane abilities, will find their test is at -2D. 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
21 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Outlaw 
The lowly in society require their Rhi for protection. However, sometimes a person will commit a great 
crime, break an oath, or even just have fled accusation of ill doings, They are now Outlaws, declared homo 
sacer, and lose all protection. Anyone may kill them with impunity, and they cannot appeal for the aegis of 
the Rhi, but this does not necessarily mean that they cannot function in society.  
An outlaw’s skills are those of stealth, force of arms, and of the forest, and when an Adventuring party 
needs the skills of a rogue, the outlaw may be the perfect choice. 
Key Attributes: Agility 
Skills: Melee, Stealth, Sleight of Hand, Marksmanship, Manipulation 
Talents: Path of the Face, Path of the Killer, Path of the Forest 
Typical Epithets: The Ra, Wind Whisperer, Stalker 
Example Prides: 
• 
You can disappear into the woods like the whisper of the wind. 
• 
Those you take from are far too wealthy already. 
• 
You committed no wrong to be outlawed.  It was all a misunderstanding, and your conscience is 
clear.  
Example Dark Secrets: 
• 
You enjoy tormenting defenceless things. 
• 
You can’t stop it once the mead is flowing. After enough drinking you tend to lose control. 
• 
Your dreams are haunted by a great stag that is dripping in blood and gore. It returns every night. 
Example Relationships:  
• 
…despises you and what you have done. They think they are better than you. 
• 
…is always moralising. They have their own faults though, and it’s best you make sure they know 
about them. 
• 
…always grabs the lion’s share when it comes to sharing the spoils. It’s time to even the score. 
Gear: A Seax, Francisca Axe, or Wooden Club, Two items of your choice from the list of Trade Goods, 
D6 Silver.

The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
22 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Trader 
The Romans have left, money is no longer minted, and settlements must be self-sufficient to survive; how-
ever, there will always be a need for someone to bring trade goods into and out of the community. Here 
the Saxon elites often best form this role, although anyone who is skilled at understanding and manipulating 
people, paying attention to the lay of the land, and repairing gear, will do well in this profession. In the 
Kingdoms of Gwynedd traders will largely barter salt, pottery6, fine textiles and crafted goods.

• 
You can convince any mark to trust you.

You are indefatigable in your pursuit of profit.

•

dom”, Ch 9,2021) 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
23 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
New Talents 
Profession Talents 
Path of the Saints 
Christian Ascetics. The first Two Ranks of Path of the Saints are identical to Path of the Forest, as the 
penitent ascetic learns the ways of the hermit. The Third Rank allows the ascetic to cast Rank 1 spells from 
any one of the seven magic disciplines of their choosing, plus the general spells. The ascetic may not use 
ingredients to increase the Power Level of their spells, as all their power comes from their deity. 
Path of the Sling 
Hunters. Acts as Path of the Arrow (FLPG pg 65), but it applies to Slings. 
Path of the Vicious Lampoon 
Bards and Druids. The Glám Dícenn is literally “the satire that destroys”; a special curse declaimed by a poet 
or druid that requires them to stand like a crane on one leg, to close one eye, and to extend one arm, before 
excoriating their target. The Glám Dícenn is a potent weapon of war, capable of blistering an opponent's 
face, causing them to be shunned by society, or even of costing them their life. 
Applying the Glám Dícenn requires spending a WP and involves an Opposed Performance (Empathy) vs 
Endurance (Strength) or Insight (Wits) test against a Human opponent. Every extra Success over that of 
the victim’s has the following effect.  
• 
Rank 1: The victim’s face bursts out in unsightly boils, which last for 2D6 days. Every extra success 
is a -1D to Empathy tests when dealing with other humans due to the victim’s unsightly appear-
ance. 
• 
Rank 2: The victim’s reputation is in tatters, which lasts for D66 days. Empathy tests when dealing 
with other humans are -2D plus -1D for every extra success.  
• 
Rank 3: Add a D8 Artefact Die to your roll. The victim is so mortally affected by the trauma that 
they take one point of Empathy damage for every extra Success. If they are Broken, the victim falls 
unconscious for D6 days (on a 6 you die on the 6th day unless Recovered by a Performance). 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
24 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Druidic Tenets and Reputation 
Druids wield great power in Gwynedd, but they are also bound by the tenets of their faith. A druid must 
be seen to follow these tenets by their peers, and a druid who wantonly breaches their vow to follow the 
tenets will be sanctioned by their druid colleagues. If a sanctioned individual does not repent, then they face 
excommunication and their soul becoming cursed, “Emendigaid”. If the druidic order declares another to be 
Emendigaid, then the druid will lose their access to their paths of magic.   
On the other hand, as a druid increases their Reputation, then they will enjoy the societal benefits befitting 
their station. If recognised, then individuals will gain the following benefits at these Reputation levels: 
• 
Reputation 3: The Druid is respected. Non-druids, including Christians but not Christian Ascetics, 
will be inclined to agree to requests by the druid and are less likely to succeed when they try to 
manipulate druids. Mechanical Effect: For Manipulation vs Insight tests, the druid adds (Reputa-
tion-2)D to their roll in addition to normal Reputation effects (FLPG pg 100).  
• 
Reputation 4: The Druid is inviolate. Non-druids, including Christians but not Christian Ascetics, 
will not willingly assault or hinder the druid.  Mechanical Effect: PCs or NPCs must pass an Em-
pathy test if they wish to harm or hinder the individual, using (Reputation-3)D negative dice7. 
• 
Reputation 5: The Druid may excommunicate others, cursing their soul. If they carry out a ritual 
of Emendigaid (pg. 153) on an individual, then the person will be fated to still enter an otherworld 
upon their death, but cursed to stay in the dark and never be reborn. Additionally, druids subject 
to this ritual will lose their access to their paths of magic. 
Druids dealing with each other use the Reputation and Manipulation rules as-is. 
Central Tenets 
Connection with Nature 
Due to the sacred interconnectedness of all living things druids seek to maintain balance in the natural 
world. 
Seasonal Celebrations 
Druids mark the turning of the seasons with appropriate ritual and celebration, honouring the cycle of life, 
death, and rebirth. 
Sacred Grove 
Druids hold certain groves as sacred spaces where they conduct rituals, share knowledge, and commune 
with the spirits of the land. 
 
 
7 i.e. Each Success on the negative dice counters on Success on the Empathy Roll. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
25 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Sanctions 
Alignment with Natural Law: 
Individuals who violate the sacred balance of nature or harm the community must be guided back to a 
position of balance. 
Spiritual Guidance 
Guidance includes education, meditation, and spiritual counselling. If this fails, then the druid must censure 
the individual, removing them from rights of religious observance and declaring them Emendigaid, or 
cursed. 
Restoration 
Emendigaid individuals may seek redemption through specific rituals, penance, and acts of restitution to 
the natural world. 
Code of Conduct 
Balance and Moderation 
Druids are bound by a code of balance and 
moderation, preventing them from using their 
powers for personal gain or causing undue 
harm. 
Respect for Free Will 
Druids are forbidden from forcefully convert-
ing others to their beliefs. They may share 
knowledge and guidance, but true under-
standing must come from personal choice. 
Non-Interference with Mortal 
Affairs: 
Druids are cautious about directly intervening 
in mortal conflicts, as they believe in the im-
portance of free will and the natural course of 
events. 
Limited Use of Destructive 
Magic: 
Druidic magic is primarily focused on healing, 
protection, and communing with nature. De-
structive magic is discouraged, and its use re-
quires justification in the name of preserving 
balance. 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
26 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Equipment & Food 
The existing Forbidden Lands Equipment and Food details (FLPG pg 179-195) is used with a few adjust-
ments. 
Coins 
Although coins stopped being minted sometime in the last 100 years and a non-money based economy has 
since become the norm, coins are still accepted for goods in the Kingdoms of Gwynned. Their liquidity 
makes them more desirable than barter. You may find truly old Celtic Stators, (more here) or merely antique 
Roman Solidus coins (more here), but they all will happily be accepted in Gwynedd. 
With respect to value, the standard Forbidden Lands monetary system (FLPG pg 36-37, 100 coppers = 10 
silvers = 1 gold coin) is used.  
Trade Goods 
Some trade goods, weapons, and armour may be sourced via the occasional Trader; however, most items 
will likely be crafted, or purchased from a craftsperson, in a Tref or Hillfort. 
Trade goods are per the FLPG chapter 9. However, Lanterns, Magnifying Glasses, and Spyglasses are 
not available. Locks exist, although most are crude wooden affairs, and there are some remaining Roman 
iron locks. Parchment exists but is much more expensive than in the FLPG pg 182, here it is 6 silver per 
sheet and Rare, rather than 6 copper per sheet and Uncommon.  
Food and drink, clothing, weapons, and protective gear are as per below. 
Food & Drink 
Barley, oats, and rye are eaten by the Eillt farmers, while wheat is generally reserved for the ruling classes. 
All these grains are consumed as bread, porridge, and gruel.  
Cheese, fruits, and vegetables are important supplements for the lower orders, while meat is reserved for 
the Uchelwyr (nobles). Mead made from bee honey is common and wine is imported and drunk only by 
the Rhi and their entourage. 
Now that starvation is biting the area, and the cost of food has now doubled, so use Forbidden Lands 
Players Guide chapter 9 standard costs times two. 
Clothing & Appearance 
Female clothing: (Saxon and Celtic) typically, an ankle length under gown fixed with a buckled leather or 
cloth belt, which held knives and tools. Additionally, women in their teens to about forty would wear over 
the top a linen or wool peplos-style gown, fixed with a pair of shoulder brooches (possibly very large and 
ornate in the case of the Celts). Often a garland of beads would be suspended between the shoulder 
brooches. For outerwear a poncho made from skins, furs, or lanolin-rich wool with a hole for the head 
would be used. Shoes, which might be dispensed with when labouring, are usually made of leather and 
bound with straps. 
Male Clothing: (Saxon and Celtic) typically, a cloak, tunic, trousers, and leggings. The cloak would nor-
mally be fur lined, whilst the cloth tunic was usually secured with a bucked belt. The trousers and leggings, 
of leather or cloth, finish the outfit. Like womenfolk, leather shoes may be dispensed with whilst labouring. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
27 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Hats, hoods, and gloves/mittens are commonly worn. In the case of the Britons, large ornate brooches are 
used to fix the overcloak, and decorative armlets and torcs might be worn. 
Druid’s Clothing: typically, druids will wear white linen or cloth robes and leather shoes. 
Tattoos: Many Britons and Gaels exhibit temporary and permanent tattoos. Woad is most often used in 
both cases – for permanent tattoos woad dye paste is forced under the skin using needle-like implements. 
The tattoos’ function is to provide protection, and, in battle, show the ferocity, courage, and lack of empathy 
of their wearer. The symbology used included single, double and triple swirls, as well as triskelions, and 
intricate geometric shapes. Battle scars are a source of pride and are proudly displayed. 
Tonsures: Druids will tend to tonsure their heads by shaving all but a tuft, or cirrus, at the front8, whilst 
Christian Ascetics will tend to shave all but a band running from ear-to-ear over their foreheads9. 
Hair and Facial Hair: The Britons tend to grow their hair long, although warriors may braid them, spike 
them with lime, or cut them short in a bowl-cut to keep the hair out of their eyes. Beards or shaved cheeks 
and moustaches are common for men, whilst women would braid and incorporate knots and buns in their 
hair, with combs made of horn or bone. 
Weapons 
The following weapons are readily available in the Kingdoms of Gwynedd. See FLPH pg 101 for Melee 
equivalents and pg 103 for Ranged equivalents. For how these weapons were used see Anglo-Saxon warfare, 
and Celtic warfare. 
France Javelin  
• 
An angon, like a Roman Pilum, with a barbed 
head and a long shank made of iron mounted 
on a wooden shaft of about 1.5m in length. 
They are used to disable an enemy’s shield and 
thus disrupt a shield wall.  
• 
FL equivalent: Short Spear in Melee, Throwing 
Spear as Ranged. Additional Ranged Feature: 
Disables Shield (on a successful parry the jave-
lin becomes stuck in the shield, rendering it 
useless for defence until repaired via Crafting) 
Seax 
• 
A short sword used by the Saxons. Its long sin-
gle-edged blade is fixed to a wood or horn hilt 
by a tang. It is much better suited to the kind 
of close-quarters combat seen in a shield wall 
than the Celtic Long Sword.  
• 
FL equivalent: Shortsword 
 
 
 
8 McIlwain, JT, “The Celtic Tonsure Revisited”, Pecia Le livre et l’écrit, pg. 63-76. https://doi.org/10.1484/J.PE-
CIA.3.5 
9 Venclova, N. “The Venerable Bede, druidic tonsures and archaeology”, Antiquity, Vol 76, Issue 292, June 2002, pp 458-471. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
28 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Spear 
• 
Features a leaf-shaped iron tip with steel along its sharpened edges and a total length of 1.8 to 2.4m 
long.  
• 
FL equivalent: Short (1.8-2.0m) or Long (2.0-2.4m) Spear 
Celtic Long Sword 
• 
Essentially a version of a Roman era spatha, a straight long sword 0.5 to 1.0m long with a handle 
length between 0.18 and 0.20m 
• 
FL equivalent: Broadsword 
Francisca Hand Axe  
• 
A light throwing axe with an arch shaped head about 0.1m long and a haft length of about 0.4m. 
• 
FL equivalent: Handaxe in Melee, Throwing Axe for Ranged. 
Slings and Bows exist, but are not normally used in combat. Slings require extensive training to use effec-
tively10 and bow-making technology in the Kingdoms is not what it is elsewhere currently. These locally 
made bows are only used for hunting.  
• 
Bows: For anyone using a locally made bow, use their normal stats from FL, but reduce their 
Bonus by 1, and treat each Range as one step longer than actual (except for Arm’s length). So, Near 
range becomes Short, Short becomes Long and Long becomes Distant. Foreign-made short and 
longbows, if they can be procured, act as per FLPG pg 188. 
• 
Slings: For anyone using a Sling, if they have the Throwing Arm Talent (see FLPG pg 82) AND 
Marksmanship Skill, then they can use the sling as per FLPG pg 188, except the maximum Range 
extends to Long11. Otherwise, all attacks are at -3D and maximum Range remains Short. 
Also available, per FLPH pg 101 and 103, are Unarmed, Knife, Wooden Club, Staff, and Rock  
No other types of weapons are available or Craftable (due to lack of knowledge, tools, and materials), unless 
from a source outside of Gwynedd, or an Artefact. 
 
 
10 Per "The sling requires enormous skill, one that can generally only be obtained with training from childhood (Hawkins, 1847; Korf-
mann, 1973; Wise, 1976; Ferrill, 1985). Without this mastery, a person armed with the weapon would be practically useless. The sling 
is exceptionally difficult to aim because it is being rotated when fired. It is common for people to fire projectiles backwards when they are 
first learning, meaning a high degree of proficiency is needed before they can be safely placed in a battlefield situation." Harrison, Chris. 
“The Sling in Medieval Europe.” The Bulletin of Primitive Technology. Vol #31, Spring 2006. 
11 Per the reference above, Slings were effective to very long ranges in the hands of skilled users.

The following protective gear are readily available, or can be Crafted, in the Kingdoms of Gwynedd. See

Shields

Oval or an elongated oval, made of wood, and usually reinforced with leather.

The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
30 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
The GM’s Framework 
The Battle of Badon took place just two years ago in 493 
CE12. King Arthur has pushed back the tide of advancing 
Saxon culture and has given a respite to the Britons in the 
West. However, in the west of Britain, in the Brythonic 
Kingdoms of Gwynedd, a mystery blight is affecting the 
land. Since the floods of last harvest there's been almost 
no rain, and whilst people starve the birth rate has plum-
meted. 
The rise of Christianity in the region has led to the waning 
of the protection previously provided by the Pagan Gods. 
As the protection wanes crops wither and die, livestock 
starve, and pagans have become infertile. When Christi-
anity is stronger everywhere, then Annwn will fade and 
the land will prosper under a new God, but in the mean-
time the people suffer and the veil between the World 
and the Otherworld grows thin. 
Although The Blight represents one problem, there are 
many possible solutions; the design of this campaign set-
ting is to use modular Obstacles13. There are three solu-
tion paths presented, but feel free to repurpose and reuse 
the Obstacles as necessary to build a compelling narra-
tive14.  
1. In The Way of Blood the PCs are attempting to deal with the Christians head on – by attempting 
to incapacitate their leader, the Abad Cadfan.  
2. In The Way of Magic the PCs are attempting to strengthen the land by finding a fabled magical 
Eaglestone, keeping it from the Dragons, and returning it to the Well of the World on Ynys Mons 
3. In The Way of Spirit the PCs are attempting to strengthen the pagan god Cernunnos by returning 
his golden torc to him – again through the Well of the World on Ynys Mons.  
To achieve any of these goals will require the PCs to navigate the political intrigues amongst the cantrefs as 
well as deal with the mundane, magical, and spirit creatures that inhabit Gwynedd and the otherworld of 
Annwn. 
 
 
 
12 The actual exact date is unknown, let alone whether the man that later stories called King Arthur was involved.  
13 See The Sharper Adventures Method, (pg. 4). 
14 See the Obstacle Map (pg. 73). 
Britain 
495 CE 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0

Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
The Setting: The Kingdom of Gwynedd 
Perched at the extremities of Britain, Gwynedd was ruled by the Romans until 407 CE, when they withdrew 
from these lands. In their place the land had Cunedda Wledig, a warlord from the north, as king. Now, 
decades later, his descendants rule a kingdom split into smaller parcels, cantrefs. These petty-kings, Rhiau, 
vie for control and influence. 
Afflogion cantref occupies a 
narrow peninsula, ruled by Rhi 
Einion Frenin, is an area where 
Christian worship is on the rise. 
The island of Ynys Enlli, at its 
tip is largely autonomous and 
ruled by the druid Morgen and 
her sisters.  
Arfon cantref, ruled by the Rhi 
Cadwallon Longhand, is the 
most prosperous of the sub-
kingdoms, largely due to the 
rich arable land on the large is-
land of Ynys Mons. Ynys Mons 
is ruled by the Sorcerer Tobre-
can on behalf of Cadwallon. 
Dunoding cantref, ruled by 
Rhi Dingad ab Eifion, is 
smaller and less prosperous 
than its neighbours, but it is sit-
uated in a strategically important 
position regarding resources 
and transport links and therefore is relatively influential. Dunoding is where the PCs nominally hale 
from and where their adventures begin.  
Rhos cantref, ruled by the ageing Rhi Owain Whitetooth, is small and less influential amongst the sub-
kingdoms, however it does benefit from rich copper mines.  
The other cantrefs, Edeyrnion and Meirionydd, do not feature politically in this setting15, but a GM could 
develop these aspects as necessary16.  
 
 
 
 
15 The In Media Res beginning starts with a foray across the notional border between Dunoding and Meironydd, but 
in a wild and unpopulated area. 
16 For more information on the Cantrefs at this period of time see https://www.historyfiles.co.uk/FeaturesBrit-
ain/CymruGwynedd.htm.

The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
32 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
The Premise 
The Blight 
A sickness stalks the land of Gwynedd. Last harvest the crops rotted on the fields and the livestock died 
where they stood. The people are becoming ill with new plagues and, more worryingly, pregnancies are 
dwindling. At this rate there will be no births next year. The locals have taken to calling it The Blight (Y 
Malltod). The winter is quickly passing; the feast of Imbolc was celebrated, but the rains have not come. 
In the real world there was a change in climate at this time to wetter and colder weather, but here the real 
cause is much more plausible to a Pagan or Christian. Viz, the recent rise of Christianity in Gwynedd has 
led to a proportionate reduction in the Celtic gods Cernunnos’ and Brigid’s power. This to the extent that 
their lapse in protection has led to The Blight. Furthermore, with their distraction, malign supernatural 
elements have begun leaking through from Annwn, the Otherworld to wreak havoc on Pagans and Chris-
tians alike. 
No one knows when exactly it started, but since Samhain17 494 (calendar) the weather has taken a turn for 
the worse, and the number of pagan births has dropped precipitously, and it seems women are no longer 
becoming pregnant. Note: It is not yet clear to the populace, but it only the pagans who are struggling to 
conceive.  
Although there have been strident calls for the 
druids to raise a wicker man, the Druids refuse 
to address the problem collectively, because 
Archdruid Bodhmall’s augury revealed that, 
should she interfere, The Blight would grow to 
affect the druids also. She has declared an in-
junction upon the druidic class of the area from 
intervening, stating that the gods’ will is not to 
be gainsaid.  
Signs of the Blight: Dying crops. Lack of signs 
of obviously pregnant women. Fouled water 
sources. Ill and starving Aillt farmers.  
The Plague 
Along with starvation and infertility associated 
with The Blight there is a new plague affecting 
the Kingdoms. The Plague is a Virulence 4 
Disease (FLPG pg 114). Rats and humans are 
the hosts and fleas and exhaled respiratory 
droplets by the sick are both vectors. The 
symptoms arise 1D6 day after exposure (Prox-
imity to ill people or their flea-ridden clothing) 
and either show as enflamed lymph nodes (bu-
boes) or a terrible cough.  
 
17 Celebrations for Saint Cynog’s day, 9th of October, led by Abad Cadfan and his monks in L3.10 Saint Cynog’s 
Monastery, proved to be the tipping point, although the Celts are unaware of this. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
33 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Emotional Stakes 
Using the Sharper Adventures method, the Players need to be invested in the outcome of their work. This 
is built into the Character Creation in The Ways by their Motivation (see Your Adventurer (pg. 10), which is 
designed to tackle the Premise of the Blight head-on but in an individual manner. 
Obstacles 
Obstacles (pg. 78) are the core of the narrative that you will build with your players. They may be “encoun-
ters” in a traditional sense, but at their most basic an Obstacle prevents the PCs from attaining their goal. 
The Obstacles are structured in terms of listing the Goals, Setting the Scene, identifying the Dilemma, 
listing the more obvious Choices, listing the Consequences to the more obvious Choices, listing any 
Rooting Interests that may influence the PC’s decisions, and then providing information about where to 
go from there, Next Steps. There are two special Obstacles, the Point of No Return and the Escalation. 
Act Break - Point of no Return  
The Point of No Return is another aspect of Sharper Adventures, and this is provided by O04. Afanc (pg. 
89). This Obstacle puts the PCs in a sequence of events that will put them in direct conflict with Cadfan 
and the Christians. Until this point the PCs will have been exploring Dunoding and getting a feel for the 
place, but their scope of effect has been relatively small, and they have not had to worry about the bigger 
picture. From this point onwards, however, the PCs are on a collision course with the political factions at 
play in Gwynedd. 
The PCs have been carrying out their tasks safe in the knowledge that they have the patronage of the Rhi. 
As the situation progresses to O05. Censure, (pg. 92) the PCs stand accused of interfering with a Christian 
Sacred Site, and they stand to lose the patronage of their Rhi, or worse. 
Act Break - The Escalation 
The second Act break in Sharper Adventures is The Escalation. Now the situation is found to be much 
more serious than first thought, and after this Obstacle there is series of incidents leading to the Resolution. 
As the PCs have three ways to choose, there are three points in the Obstacle Map (pg.79) where the Escalation 
Occurs.  
1. If following The Way of Blood, it is O19. Maelgwn’s Downfall (pg. 132) when the PC’s plan goes 
awry and the Artefact that they sought to use to remove the Blight causes the prince Maelgwn to 
run amok. 
2. If following The Way of Magic, it is O18. Anger the Dragon (pg 129) when the PCs raise the ire of 
the Dragons of Gwynedd whilst attempting to recover the Eaglestone Amulet. 
3. If following The Way of Spirit, it is O17. Caer Sidi and Custennin (pg. 125) when the PCs summon 
the spinning fortress of glass, Caer Sidi, from the otherworld of Annwn. 
From this point onwards the PCs solution to the issue of the Blight is fixed, even if the path they take is 
not.  
The Resolution: 
Once the PCs have completed the task on their chosen Way, then the resolution is the same – a return to 
the Status Quo, for now. However, new adventures can await your Players, either in Gwynedd or the other 
Kingdoms of Britain. See Further Reading (pg. 191) for more sources of information. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
34 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
GMing Mood Prompts 
The following prompts can be used to help the GM visualise and describe the locations encountered by the 
PCs. 
Omens and Portents 
□ An ungainly heron lumbers into the still air, you stop to watch which direction it will go. It bears 
right and you heave a sigh of relief. 
□ A flock of starlings darts and weaves, as the small birds search out a safe roost for the night. Your 
heart sinks as they circle you anti-sunward. 
□ A hare bolts from the grass beside you, first running on the road and then through the fields.  A 
feeling of uncertainty is overwhelming – how should you interpret this omen? 
□ Ahead a ghost fence18 heaves into view – jawless skulls clack in the breeze on the thin wooden 
posts, creating a barrier that only a druid would cross.  
Trefs and Farms 
□ Smoke rises through the thatch of sturdy roundhouses, as small children squeal and yelp in their 
games about the outside. 
□ A suspicious farmer eyes you, as she pulls back the gate and grunts a poor welcome into the pali-
sade.  
□ A pig squeals as it is dragged through the muck towards the covered slaughtering area. 
□ Oxen strain against a huge plough, whilst a young boy beats their flanks with a stick. 
□ Chickens cluck and scatter as you approach. 
□ A cross, woven out of rushes, hangs over the door, signifying this Christian house is under protec-
tion.  
Sacred Groves 
□ The calls of birds fall silent as you pass between the line of low standings stones marking the edge 
of the grove. The air feels different here, like all your senses are sharper somehow. 
□ The sound of creaking alerts you to several bundles of what appears to be animal entrails that are 
slowly swaying overhead, hung from a huge oak’s branches. 
□ The tree boughs sway and the leaves flicker, but no breeze kisses your cheeks. 
□ The tracks underfoot make no sense – large and small animals appear to have traversed this grove, 
but inexplicably you cannot determine where they came from or where they went.  
□ The path widens and you realise somehow you have been turned around again. Yet again you have 
reached the same clearing, gained by ducking through an arch of braided rowan boughs. In the 
centre of the clearing is a low, lichen-covered stone with a rough hollow on its flat top. 
 
 
18 See Omens and Oaths (pg. 109). 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
35 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Craggy Hillforts 
□ The harsh wind whips your breath away. Up here heather fights with large rock boulders for dom-
inance, and the view is clear but bleak. 
□ A chatter of red-billed choughs sweeps past, their ‘chee-ow’ call reflecting oddly off the cyclopean 
blocks of the fortress. 
□ A guard stares at you impassively from the top of the stone wall as you approach, only bothering 
to call out their challenge as your hand rises to push at the heavy oak gate. 
□ Out of the wind you immediately feel warmer, although this roundhouse’s thatch is old, black on 
the inside with creosote deposits, and needing replacement – the smoke, then, does not dissipate, 
and is thick within and catches at the back of your throat. 
□ A gate has been left untied, and it swings in the breeze, clattering against wet stones. 
□ A group of Ambaxtos Warriors stand around the fire, looking over their shoulders at you – Their 
demeanour is half-contempt, half-curious, and their huge Cú Faoil wolfhounds gnaw and crack pig 
bones at their feet.  
Windswept Coasts 
□ The water surges and roars, white foam lashing the rocks, whilst the spray soaks you – chilling you 
to the bone. 
□ With a slop and a gurgle, whatever it was that was following you drops under the water’s surface, 
and all is quiet. 
□ The oars pull rhythmically against the water, and slowly you begin to make headway against the 
current. Straggly kelp washes by, reminding you uncomfortably of how fast the rip is here. 
□ The sun begins to set, briefly lighting the sky on fire with a wash of orange, deep pink, and then 
purple, as a green flash is briefly seen the moment the sun disappears into the sea. 
□ A gull cries overhead and a smelly white splash appears on your tunic. You’ve had better days. 
Boggy Swamps 
□ Mosquitos buzz and the squelching of your shoes sucking out of the mud is followed by a bloom 
of the released marsh-gas stench. 
□ The ground underfoot begins to rise and becomes firmer. However, you realise to your dismay, 
that this is just an island in an extensive morass. This will take some time. 
□ Out of the corner of your eye you spot movement, but when you turn and look the scene is static. 
It was probably just a branch floating on the ever-present muck that is everywhere here. 
□ “Peewit! Peewit! Peewit!” the black and white bird with the distinctive swept-up crest and round wing 
shape dashes back and forth, up and down. Something has got it perturbed and it is not you.  
□ This corner of the marshland seems to look exactly like every other. It is time to admit that you 
are lost.  
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
36 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Roman Remnants 
□ Formerly flush paved avenues, now uneven, with weeds thrusting upwards and large stone flags 
cracked in twain make your progress slow. 
□ Uncannily straight sided stone and brick buildings are roofed in clay tiles. However, many of the 
roof tiles are missing, and some roofs have completely collapsed. 
□ Inside tiled floors retain some of their mosaics, but in many areas the footing is uncertain with 
small, coloured stones missing from the previously intricate representations of the hunting of ani-
mals, of battles and abstract shapes. 
Senex Vaie (the Old Roads) 
□ Atonal music leeches through the firmament. No breeze stirs. A smell of strange fruits becomes 
almost overwhelming. 
□ Ducking through the arch of woven willow boughs, the light seems to change. 
□ A crow alights on a tall, runically carved standing stone and regards you with one intelligent-seem-
ing eye.  
□ The path is distinct, but to the side far more interesting sights appear. A friendly face gives a wink, 
the smell of newly baked bread assaults your nose, and enticing views of idyllic glades assail the 
senses.
