“This mist is not like others… no, when normal mists come down and then burn off 
we don’t find cattle sliced up into small pieces. Be careful when you go out when the 
murk rises my friends. Be careful.” 
 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
169 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Factions & Politics 
The advisors to the Rhiau have been colluding in secret to keep the peace between the Cantrefs. For a 
faction map, as it stands at the beginning of the campaign, see the Timeline & Faction Map (pg 80)  
The Rhiau are “bound by conventions of honour, reciprocity, and political pragmatism”110. They are also constrained 
by a taxation system where goods, rather than money, are rendered. Some of these goods are perishable 
and some not so useful to the Rhi (reed for thatching houses can only be used in a limited number of ways). 
At the same time a Rhi would attract Ambaxtos to his warband only by being seen to offer a chance for 
wealth and glory. So, the Rhiau would feast their followers, lavish on them gifts, and give them the oppor-
tunity for war and spoils of war. Cattle represent high value commodities that are also highly mobile, so 
cattle raids on your neighbours are a good way to keep your Ambaxtos happy – something necessary if you 
are going to keep your position of power with respect to these same neighbours.  
Annwn 
Annwn is one of the Otherworlds. Although it is the land of the fae, this is the not the land of the Spencer's 
Faerie Queen, it is the abode of fae, yes, but also of gods and of the dead.  
Annwn is accessed via the Senex Vaie (pg. 170), the old roads, and ruled by Gwyn ap Nudd, Master of the 
Wild Hunt. Its residents include Spirits (See Beings of Annwn (pg. 146)), Brythonic and Gaelic Gods (See 
Deities (pg 150)), Monsters, and the Prematurely Dead (such as Morgen’s(C2.4) brother Mordred). 
Although many of those who the PCs encounter in the lands of Annwn are fair, or at least not foul, the 
rare Monsters can be truly hellish. “A wide-mawed beast bearing a hundred heads”, “A black-groined toad bearing a 
hundred claws”, and “a mottled, ridged, serpent with a hundred souls, by their sin tortured in the folds of its flesh” may all 
be found111.  
There can be no detailed map of Annwn, or at least none that would be useful on a repeat visit. The 
landscape and even the buildings change in their own mysterious ways and on their own schedule. The 
dwellings of the fae “are not only located either underground, in hills, caves or burial mounds, in a glass fortress, but also 
beyond or under the sea, in lakes, beyond the waters, even in the mists, but also in woodlands, in wastelands and wilderness 
and, last but not least, in people’s imaginations”112 
Neither the sun nor moon rises here, there being an unvarying lightness to the purple sky, and there are no 
seasons, adverse weather, or even changes in the temperature. Time runs differently here than mortal realms 
– sometimes much quicker, sometimes glacially. 
Annwn is also a place of lavish feasts with rich food and drink, but those who sample this fare may find 
themselves unable to return to the mortal world.  
Generally, mortals who venture here will find it bewildering, intoxicating, and extremely dangerous – and 
not necessarily in obvious ways. 
 
 
110 The First Kingdom, by Max Adams.  
111 From “The Battle of the Trees”, Lyfr Taliesin. 
112 “Y Tylwyth Teg, An Analysis of a Literary Motif”, Angelika Heike Rüdiger, 2021. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
170 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Senex Vaie 
“The Old Ways” in Latin, these are paths in Annwn that link sacred sites (nodes) in the real world and offer 
a quick transit between far flung locations in Gwynedd. They also can be used to travel to Annwn and back. 
The traverse though is not without peril, even more so if travelling to Annwn, and the ways can only be 
opened reliably by a user of magic. However, now that the Blight has gripped the land, The Ways have 
taken to opening randomly to a schedule to their own capricious purpose (When visiting a sacred site with 
a node roll a D66. On a 66 the Ways open of their own accord).  
When a one of the Senex Vaie opens, there is a perceptible shimmer and the landscape surrounding the 
site, be it standing stones, a giant’s grave, or neolithic barrow, will subtly alter. Perhaps there is now a brake 
of rowan nearby that wasn’t noticed before, or a new path in the forest appears.  
If someone wanders into this new feature, then they enter the Otherworld of Annwn. They must not tarry 
and if they stray off the path, then the magic 
of the fae may take them; they may return out-
wardly, or inwardly, utterly changed, and they 
may not return for many days, yet seemingly 
only moments will have passed for them. 
For those opening the Senex Vaie with a spell, 
the destination node they seek is obvious to 
them, although they still risk wandering off 
the path (see below). For those wandering 
into a random opening of the Senex Vaie 
without the assistance of magic then they will 
find themselves exiting at a random Senex 
Vaie Node within the Kingdoms of Gwyn-
edd. 
All those travelling on the Senex Vaie should roll Move or Insight (the PC’s choice) to ensure they stay 
on the Way.  
• 
On a Success then the traveller has been able to negotiate the Ways safely and they reach their 
intended destination.  
• 
If a traveller gets more than one Success, they can use each extra Success to keep one companion 
from wandering off the Ways.  
• 
On a Failure the traveller wanders off the Way and they must roll on the Senex Vaie mishap table.  
• 
Banes act normally.  
• 
If you are broken whilst travelling on the Senex Vaie, then it is as if you rolled a 6 on the Senex 
Vaie Mishap Table. 
If a traveller is using the Senex Vaie to travel to Annwn, then they must automatically roll on the Senex 
Vaie Mishap Table. 
Note. The travelling roll may be Pushed and only one roll is needed no matter how many segments there 
are to the journey. Activities such as holding hands adds 1D to rolls and tying each other together with 
rope would add 2D; however, even tightly bound rope may find itself unaccountably untying itself in the 
Fae lands.  


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
171 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Senex Vaie mishap table: 
• 
On a 1-3: you soon return to the Way but are mentally changed. 1 Attribute point must be swapped 
between Wits and Empathy, but you may choose which way. Describe your New Personality  
• 
On a 4-5: you soon return to the Way but are physically changed. 1 Attribute point must be 
swapped between Strength and Agility, but you may choose which way. Describe your new look.  
• 
On a 6: you stray so far off the Way that you do not find it again. You Return in d66 days as an 
NPC and completely changed. 
Knowledge of the Senex Vaie: Druids and Sorcerers will know of the existence of Senex Vaie and know 
that traversing them is considered extremely dangerous. Other professions may carry out a Lore test at -2D 
to see if they have the same knowledge. 
List of Senex Vaie Nodes 
L1.2 Ffynhonnell Aberdaron, Afflogion 
L2.2 Holy Well, Ynys Enlli 
L3.4 Carnedd y Cawr, Arfon 
L3.5 Cwm Dyli, Arfon 
L3.7 Llyn Gwynant and Caer Sidi, Arfon 
L4.2 Bryn Ceilli Ddu, Ynys Mons 
L4.3 Llwyn o Gerrig, Ynys Mons 
L5.3 Bryn Cader Faner, Dunoding 
L5.8 Moel Yscydion, Dunoding

Weather 
The Weather is affected by The Blight, so whilst it is active there should be no, or little, rainfall in Gwynedd. 
If desired, use the Weather Hex Flower to determine the prevailing weather each day.

opposite side (e.g. Hard Frost goes to 
Overcast on a 7). 
If you hit a line, the weather repeats.

The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
173 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Omens and Oaths 
The citizens of the Kingdoms of Gwynedd are superstitious to an extreme degree. Everyday events which 
may be interpreted as good- or ill-omens include, and will be widely known to the PCs: (Also see here) 
You will have good fortune if you: 
• 
See a hare running off the road 
• 
Encounter a toad on the road 
• 
Find a needle (though your luck will turn if you dispose of the needle) 
You will have bad fortune if you: 
• 
Encounter a hare on the road 
• 
Encounter a raven or Magpie 
• 
Encounter a Christian Ascetic or a Druid 
General 
• 
The direction of flight of birds, escaping hares, and the like are favourable if they move on the 
right (destra) and unfavourable on the left (sinistra) 
• 
Spitting can drive out contagion and evil spirits 
• 
If a Raven calls 5 times the weather will change 
• 
In a party of 13, the first to get up will die within the year 
• 
A dog howling in a house means someone there soon will die 
• 
If you hear a cuckoo call five times, then you have just five years to live 
• 
When the spirits of the Otherworld are present, then loud noises can drive them away 
• 
Ghost Fences, made of human bones by druids and sorcerers, and sometimes just two skulls 
facing each other, are ill-omened to cross (see here). Ghost Fences have a Fear Attack rating, 
which applies to any non-druid who tries to cross them. The attack rating doubles if one tries to 
damage or remove the fence. Druids can remove Ghost Fences but to do so, or to allow an ally to 
do so, would constitute breaking of their tenets, and should come with significant in-game backlash 
from the druidic order if it becomes known.  
• 
Druids will circle their groves “sunwise” (clockwise), always keeping the grove on their right, when 
they approach, as to do otherwise is ill-omened. 
• 
Once you have sucked an egg out of its shell, break the shell so to avoid becoming spell bound. 
• 
Warriors will whet their weapons in good-omened places and never in their own shadow. 
The Britons take a great store in Oaths. An example oath could be "I swear by Cernunnos by whom my people 
swear. If I break my oath, may the land open to swallow me, the sea rise to drown me, and the sky fall upon me"113. 
 
 
 
113 An example of the threefold death suffered by kings, heroes and gods. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
174 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
The Law 
The Welsh Laws at this time are unclear but are presumed to be largely the same as the later Cyfraith Hywel, 
which were based upon oral tradition from this time. The laws are enforced in each Cantref by a group of 
Uchelwyr nobles on behalf of the Rhi. 
Most law is based around the Galanas, an idea similar to the Saxon Weregild, where a killer must pay a sum 
to the family of the victim (or the owner in the case of Slaves), based upon the victims status in society. 
Assault had a similar type of price, sarhaed, rape was considered theft and remedied by another fine, dirwy, 
and theft itself was punished with imprisonment or hanging. 
During a trial the accused could be declared innocent via Trial-by-Oath, whereby they would gather as many 
men as possible, usually 11 or 12, to take an oath as to their innocence. The exception here were oaths by 
the Naw Tafiodiog, “The Nine Tongued-ones”. These are special witnesses whose oath alone could be taken 
at face-value114.  
“Nine persons who are to be believed in their testimony, each one of them separately on his oath.  
1. A lord between his two men as to a suit which they acknowledge to have been previously before him ; and he be not 
interested in the suit, and they be not in agreement as to the mode.  
2. An abbot between his two monks on the threshold of the choir.  
3. A father between his two sons by placing his hands on the head of the son against whom he shall swear, and saying 
thus: 'By God, the One who created me thy father and thee my son, the truth I declare between you.'  
4. A judge as to what he previously decided, if the two persons concerning whom he judged are disputing concerning the 
decision.  
5. A surety as to his suretyship115 if he admit a part and deny another part.  
6. A priest between his two parishioners as to the testimony which was testified to him.  
7. A virgin as to her virginity, if the man to whom she was given declares she was not a virgin in order to take away 
her right and her due ; or if she is violated and the man who violated her says she was not a virgin, the virgin's 
testimony is to be believed against him.  
8. A shepherd of a hamlet (trefgoad) as to his shepherding if one animal kills the other.  
9. A thief without hope of mercy concerning his fellow-thief, when brought to the gallows ; because credible is his word 
concerning his companions and the chattels they thieved, without a relic ; and his companion is not to be destroyed on 
his word, but is to be a thief for sale.  
To be believed also is a contract man as to his contract.  
And so also to be believed is an informer who gives a full information.  
And a giver of property is to be believed as to the chattels he gives, and so it is said: 'There is no gift except by consent.'” 
Trial by Combat is still an option.  
If the Galanas, Sarhaed, or Dirwy was not paid, then the accused was liable to be made Outlaw. 
Outlawry: Once made outlaw, then all legal protection was withdrawn, and anyone was legally empowered 
to persecute or kill the outlaw. If they did, however, then they were advised to declare this as soon as 
possible, as the outlaw’s family might then bring together enough witnesses to overturn their original out-
lawry. and thus make the killer subject to Galanas payment. 
 
114 The Nine Tongued Ones, from the Laws of Hywel. 
115 Put another way, “a giver on his gift” 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
175 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Flavour Scenes 
Aspects you may include that evoke the feel of the time without necessarily being core to the narrative 
include: 
□ The PCs evoking great tension when they bring weapons into the feasting hall of a Rhi. 
□ Druids stalking inviolate between two armed factions as they clash. They are safe in the knowledge 
that no-one will touch them for fear of being cursed in the Otherworld. 
□ Ambaxtos Warriors collecting taxes on behalf of a weak Rhi and taking bribes to downplay the 
extent of the harvest (and thus the amount of collected tax). 
□ The Rhi feasting his dependents and providing gifts such as rings and torcs116. 
□ Witnessing the cremation of a Pagan or the burial of a Christian who has died of the plague. 
□ A reiving party from one Cantref raiding its neighbours for valuable cattle. 
□ Witnessing a Saxon slave being exchanged for goods and food. 
□ Taking part in one of the Eight Festival Days (See Festivals in Gwynedd (pg. 150)) 
□ Noticing abandoned and crumbling hillforts scattered about the landscape.  
□ Coming across the burnt out remains of a Wicker Man, a site of sacrifice. 
□ Witnessing a tref creating a Need-Fire; to protect themselves from the plague. This involves dows-
ing all fires in the area and kindling a bonfire with a fire-drill rather than striking an iron with flint 
into tinder. This would be used to rekindle the hearth fires in the tref. 
 
 
 
116 Reciprocal arrangements meant that, although the people paid taxes to the Rhi, the Rhi also was dependent on the 
people. 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0 
176 
Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Names 
Suggested period appropriate names and associated pronunciation guides are provided, however, if that 
that seems non-fun, then use and pronounce names however you like.  
Brittonic Names 
It is presumed here that modern Welsh versions of ancient Briton’s names also use modern Welsh pronun-
ciation. If you want to have a go at using Welsh pronunciation, then some brief information is provided 
and an attempt at English pronunciation equivalents.  
For a primer on pronouncing Welsh names in the Arthurian Age try Sean Poage’s guide here.  
In modern Welsh the emphasis is generally on the second syllable. To give you a feel for what it sounds 
like try reading the following117: 
Ai hop ddat yw can ryd ddys and ddat yt meiks sens tw yw. Iff yw can ryd ddys, dden yw sawnd 
ryt and ar redi tw gow tw wals. Gwd lwc and haf ffyn. 
It should have sounded thus: 
I hope that you can read this, and that it makes sense to you. If you can read this, then you sound 
right and are ready to go to Wales. Good luck and have fun. 
The phonetic versions provided below are very approximate and do not attempt to reproduce the double 
L sound accurately118. To all speakers of the Welsh language I apologise for mangling it! 
Brittonic Female 
Adwent (ad-WENT) 
 
 
Afan (ah-VAN)  
 
Afandreg (ah-VAHN-dreg) 
 
Affrella (ah-FRESH-ah)  
 
Ananan (ah-NA-nahn)  
 
Anawfedd (ah-NOW-vehth)

Arianell (ahr-EE-ahn-esh) 
 
Arianfagl (ahr-EE-ahn-vah-guhl)  
Arianrhod (ahr-EE-ahn-rod) 
 
Arianwen (ahr-EE--ahn-wen) 
 
Banhadlwedd (bahn-HAD-loo-eth)

117 After Go4awalk.com. 
118 To say the ‘LL’ you can try putting the tip of your tongue against the roof of your mouth, as in starting to say a 
word starting with ‘L’ and blow out of the side of your mouth whilst smiling. Alternatively, you can try a hissed ‘SH’ 
sound.

Saxon Names 
Pronunciation Guide119: 
‘eo' as in ‘Mode’  
‘æ’ as in 'hat' 
 
a 
'a' as in 'father'  
 
‘c’ before 'e' or 'i' pronounced 'ch', otherwise pronounced 'k' 
‘g’ before 'e' or 'i' at beginning of word pronounced as a 'y', between vowels pronounced as a 'w', 
otherwise pronounced like modern 'g' 
‘ge’ the initial 'ge' is usually voiceless 
‘s’ is 'z' in middle of word, 's' at beginning or end of word 
‘f’ is 'v' in middle of word, 'f' at beginning or end of word 
‘h’ at beginning of word, as 'h' in hound, otherwise like 'ch/sh' 
‘sc’ is 'sh' 
 
‘hw’ is 'wh' 
 
 
-‘ht’ is 'ght' 
‘cw’ is 'qu' 
 
-‘ycg’ is 'idge' 
 
 
‘ecg’ is 'edge'

The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0

Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Further Reading 
Sources of information and further inspiration: 
• 
“The Buried Giant”, Novel, by Kazuo Ishiguro, 2015. 
• 
“The Winter King”, Novel, by Bernard Cornwell, 1995. 
• 
“The First Kingdom”, nonfiction, by Max Adams, 2021. 
• 
Internet Archaeology, Issue 48, Iron Age Settlement in Wales, https://intarch.ac.uk/journal/is-
sue48/ 
• 
Aurochs Substack for more detail on Arthur than is good for anyone: https://substack.com/@au-
rochs 
• 
The Roman Britain website: https://www.roman-britain.co.uk/  
• 
The Megalithic website by Andy Burnham: https://www.megalithic.co.uk/ 
• 
The RCAHMW’s website: https://coflein.gov.uk/ 
• 
“Spurrell’s Welsh-English Dictionary”, 12th Edition, 1934. (For archaic Welsh words) 
• 
“A Welsh Classical Dictionary, People in History and Legend up to about AD 1000”, Peter C 
Bartrum, National Library of Wales, 1993. 
• 
“Porius: A Romance of the Dark Ages”, by John Cowper Powys, 1951. 
• 
“Sir Gawain and the Green Knight / Pearl / Sir Orfeo”, English translation by JRR Tolkien, 1975. 
• 
“The Maginogion” and “The Book of Taliesin” Medieval Welsh Prose Poems. 
• 
Angelika Heike Rüdiger’s PhD Thesis “Y Tylwyth Teg, An Analysis of a Literary Motif” (2021) 
for a truly deep dive into Annwn, Caer Wydyr, Caer Sidi, and the like. 
For a wealth of setting material, far more than is provided here, then I can recommend the Mythic Britain 
Sourcebook for the Mythras System by Laurence Whitaker at The Design Mechanism. 
 
 


 
 
The Ways of Blood, Magic, and Spirit, v1.0

Stephen Dosman (@Dosmania) 
Acknowledgements 
Many thanks to the Creators of the Forbidden Lands ruleset, Fria Ligan, the playtesters, Phil Brunner, 
Matt Johnston, and Brian Wherry. New spells with help from Matt Johnston. Proofreading by Phil 
Brunner. 
Credits and Copyright 
This product was created under license. Forbidden Lands and its logo, are trademarks of Fria Ligan AB. 
This work contains material that is copyright Fria Ligan AB and/or other authors. Such material is used 
with permission under the Community Content Agreement for Free League Workshop. 
Maps 
1. The Cantref Map and Ynys Enlli map are based on OpenStreetMap (CC BY-SA 2.0) data. 
2. The Britain 495 CE map, is adapted from “Britain, c.540, in the time of Gildas” by Notuncurious 
(CC BY-SA 3.0) 
3. Main Gwynedd Hexmap created by Hexfriend. 
4. Tre’r Ceiri, “Dinas Affaraon” (Dinas Emrys), and “Saint Cynog’s” (Caer Seion) hillfort back-
grounds have been adapted from material in “An Inventory of the Ancient Monuments in Caernavron-
shire”, Vol II Central (1960) and Vol III West (1964), RCAHMWM (Originally Crown Copyright, 
but now Public Domain)(Shop). 
5. The Dragon’s Lair and Mine maps by Watabou’s Cave Generator. 
6. Remaining maps have been created using Roll20’s DungeonScrawl. Some maps contain Digital 
Assets from 2 Minute Tabletop (CC BY-NC-4.0), Angry Golem Games (CC BY-NC-4.0), DaeDae 
(CC BY-4.0), Daniel F Walthall (CC BY-NC-4.0), and Mark Gosbell (CC0). 
Names and Pronunciations 
1. Brythonic names are sourced from “A Welsh Classical Dictionary, People in History and Legend 
up to about AD 1000”, Peter C Bartrum, National Library of Wales, 1993. 
2. Old-Irish name pronunciations are as suggested by Annie Loughlin. 
3. Saxon Names are from Abaroth (CC BY-NC 4.0) 
Fonts 
1. Celtic Hand, by Dieter Steffmann,  
2. Celtica, by Wojciech Kalinowski (SIL OFL 1.1),  
3. Mabinogi Celtic, by Dyfed Lloyd Evans, and 
4. Garamond. 
Other 
1. The Weather Mechanic on page 172 is after “Weather Hexes” by Goblin’s Henchman
